The Japanese ‘Government iy always in a pell-mell. ‘Without trying

Recently China has’ been united under a Central Government in

Tokyo.

headed by the Shogun who 1s

* really the ruler of the country.

's replaced by,the rule of the mill-

tarists tends in one way. or another

attempt of Japan to bring China to
her. knees
Nome things more dismal are for th.
euuting

to whom. every national ‘of his
country owes his or her allegiance,

tion !s carried out. The Emperor

JAPANESE FOREIGN OFFICE.

Tokyo, Sept. 28: The Japanese Forelgn Office spokesman to-day sald that Reuter's’ ‘reports on
the. Canton bdmbings were most exaggerated which, the, spokesman explained,» was due to Reuter's’

Canton representative being a Chinese.

Reuter declared that their Canton, ‘correspondent was ‘an

Englishman whose. reports on the: bombings there ‘called for: no corrections »

Japanese press reports state from Italian, sources that British planes bombed defenceless natives
in, Tanganyika, There is positively ‘no foundation whatsoever fot such accusations.

«The Japanese Naval authorities have denied thatia Japanese submarine torpedoed Chinese junks
in South China waters. The Japanese Naval authorities declared that the German Uner Scharnhorst
which picked up ten survivors was in Manila yesterday and so could not have been In Hong Kong.
Lioyd’s agent was Informed by Reuter’s correspondent in London that the Scharnhorst ata in fact

The “iucky escape of a Chinese 5 cliseus Cantos near the ‘Bocea Tigris
Forts yesterday morning, as witnessed by Several passengers on
the rly ex boat Fatshan, was told to a reporter of the “Daily Press”
when ‘the, vessel arrived in Hong Kong in the afternoon. °

Just before the Fatshan left Canton passengers heard the air’
“raid alarm but although they scanned the sky all the way down
the river they failed to see any. ralders, oF. even, for that matter,

* any Chinese planes.

When the Fatshan was rainy the vicinity of the Bocta ©
Tigris Forts, however, they heard the drone. of & plane, and
shortly after, a Japanese machine came into view. The. raider
madeé straight for a Chinese gunboat anchored in the river and
dropped a bomb which fell amidships but failed to explode!’

“So true was the‘alm of the Japanese alrman.” said our in-
formant, “that had the bomb expladed it certainly would have

been the end of the gunboat.”

The Chinese vessel replied with machine gun fire which, how-
ever, did not seem to have any effect on the ‘plane. FF

- After ‘dropping the bomb the j

plane made off in the direction of
the coast.

JAPANESE SEARCH CHINESE
ENVOY ON BRITISH VESSEL '
Nanking, Sept. 28: An Incident
involving’ a Chinese official repre-
sentative being forcibly searched
by Japanese police on board a
British vessel has just Been
.brought to Ught.
According to information emanat-

a ing from diplomatic circles, Mr.

Chang Fu-yun,.a. councillor at-'

’ tached’ to the Chinese Delegation

to the Coronation of King George
VI and Queen Elizabeth, ‘was re-
turning to China by way of the
United.States and Japan aboard
the. Empress" of Japan. When the
vessel reached Yokohama on Sept.
18, a number, ‘of Japanese police
boarded the shin and demanded
that Mr. Chang’s baggage and
personal . belongings be searched,
despite the fact that the Chinese
envoy ‘remained. on board during
the vessel’s stopover in port.
When the Chinese Conswi-Gene-
“pal at Yokohama protested against
the Milegal search, a foreign affairs

sectional chief of the Japanese po-

lice bureau declared that he had
reecived orders to make the search
ana that during -present S!no-

‘ Japanese hostilities Chinese clvi- |

tans and officials, even Ambassa-
dors and Consuls, will he subjected
to the same treatment when
necessary. He added that “China
could also adopt such retallatory:
measures” {f she wanted. .

Despite the strong protest lodged
by Mr. Haw Shih-ying, Chinese
Ambassador to ‘Tokyo, to the’
‘Japanese Foreign Office, the fricl-,
dent is still unsettled.

“Tt is the. concensus of opinion

My here that since China and Japan

are apparently . maintaining
‘normal diplomatic relations the.
search of official representatives is
iNegul and not in accordance with

, International Law.—Gentral News

“Directing motion pictures as a

warning.

medicine, the training ts long.
arduous: and often disheartening.
The rewards are equally gteat ‘for
those, who succeed, but the oP-,
portunities: are far Jess."

Miss’ Arzner served an ap-
prenticeship of seven years as a,
stenographer, script girl, | film |
editor and scenarist, before attain.
ing her goal as director. In a.pro-
fession which boasts less than a
hundred top-flight directors, she
has maintained a position of
leadership. for eleven ‘years, and is
the first woman to do so. :

directing aS a’ lifetime Job and is |
willing to devote her entire atten-
tlon ‘to learn every detail of the
intricate technique, can hope. to
make good,’ said.the Metro-Gold.
wyn-Mayer director. “It will take
from seven to ten'years to do this, !
and necessitates a thorough study
of every phase of the industry. ”

‘TORPEDO DEPARTMENT

Commander J. H. Allison, after a
short .course in H.M.S. Vernon,
joinedon Sept. 14, the staff of the
‘Torpedoes and Mining | Department, |
Admiralty, He was. pramoted {n
the midsummer Ust after being
‘of HMS. Queen
Elizabeth, flagship in the Mediter
ranean, Commander, Allison enter-

* ed Osborne ag a cadet In Septem -

ber, 1915, was promoted to Neu-

tenant In 1924, and appo'nted to] |,

specialize in’ torpedoes in January.
1927, After a commission as tor--
pedo officer of the Wallace ‘and Sth
Destroyer Flotilla he served’ from
1931 to 1932 at the Chemical De-
fence Experimental Station, Potton,
and later in the Vernon, torpedo
school.’ He was fleet torpedo offl- j

TRAFFIC ‘COLLISION

ee

| Police Officer ‘Sustains |

Fractured Rib

A collision between a motorcycle
ridden by Police Sergeant J. &.
Riddell and a public motor car,
driven by Hung Sam, occurred at
the junction of Hennessy, Road
and Luard Road about 1.15 a.m,
yesterday,

Tt appears thut-the public car
iwhich ‘1s owned by “the Leighton
' Garage, was driving westward
(with two passengers, when the
cycle came south from Luard
Road and hit the rear of the car.

Hung swerved and crashed against’

a stone plilar on the side of the
road.”

Sergeant Riddell was flung off
his cycle, and was taken to the
Queen Mary Hospital, ‘where it was
thought he had sustained a frac-
tured rib. Both the motorcycle
ind car were badly: damaged.

| Police’ Called On Board BI.
Talma

According to “Chinese ' Press re-
Ports, there’ was a riot on board
the BI. ss, Talma, among some of
the 150 deportees brought, here
from Singapore yesterday.

Police were called on board and
it 1s understood that they found
several injured people. ,

‘The deportees, many of them:

reckless, spirited and resent{u) of
discipline, had been taken aboard
at Singapore, 150 in number, ‘in
the custody.of 21 Indian guards,
When the Talma came ..alongside
at Kowloon Wharves yesterday and
the deportees were mustered to
there was "a certain
amount of confusion and excite-
ment, Some of the deportees. were
"already on the deck, under guard,
when a’ sudden hubbub broke out
below decks in the Talma, It was
later discovered that several of- the
men had’ attacked their guards.

The. deportees have ‘since been
brought under guard’ to the Water
‘Police, Station, =»

UNMANIFESTED CARGO

Opening.'for the Crown, Mr. M.
J. Abbott, Assistant Crown Solicl-
tor, declared that accused joined,
the Imports and Exports Depart-
ment ag a, second class revenue
officer in July, 1932. His duties, as
regards unmanifested cargo, were
to seize {t, take it’ back to the Im-.
ports and Exports Department,
and to detain’ or arrest the im-
porter, if possible.

Each revenue officer was ptovid-

corded any unmanifested cargo
selzed, On the day of the alleged
offence, accused made an entry
in his diary of having supervised
the search of the Wo Ping Yee and
her passengers, but made, no men-
tlon of unmanifested cargo.

The steamer arrived from - Wu- |
chow about 5 p.m. on June 23. A
party of revenue officers, among
whom were C.R.Os, 110, 91, 80, 8

‘and 70, all in charge of accused,

went on board. C.R.O, 110 found
100 bags of wolfram ore. ‘He made
certain enquiries, as a. result of
which a man handed him a piece
of paper on which was: written,
something {n English. " .

Unable to understand what it
was, he handed it to C.R.O, 80 who
went off with it. Ultimately accused
got hold of It because C.R.O, 8 and
70, who were. in the compradore’s |,
office’ with him, would say that
they heard him remark that the
paper was false or incorrect. In
‘the office were also Li Kwok, ‘the
purser, and. ORO. ot.”

Accused asked’ the purser about
‘the falsity of the paper and thé
latter agreed 1t was false and did
net belong to the Company. The
purser further pointed out that
the document did not have the
signature of the Captain nor the
Customs chop. The Import and {-

MR ‘HY. HODSON ‘DISCUSSES

"MEETING. OF . HITLER:
- AN Dd IL: DUCE —

German Policy Is. To
Keep Italy As A.
_ Mediterranean Power =

oe! 4“

SOME REFLECTIONS

“Some reflections on the meeting that has just taken place

between Herr Hitler andi Sigrior Mussolini,” was the title of the

talk, in the weekly World Affairs

series broadcast from Daven-

_ try Last night, the speaker this time belng Mr. H. V. Hodson, 50.
well-known to wireless fans all over the’ English-speaking world,

Opening his talk, “Mr,
said that during the past few days’
the press of Central Europe wis
filled with one subject ‘to the ex-
clusion of everything'else. To the
“average man of the world: the

seHlous turn of events in the» Far'

East, and even the Spanish. civil
war, might have been a deal more
important, "but to the people of
Italy and Germany nothing” could
possibly have: been more important
than the meeting at Munich. Was
it because the two chief actors were
dictators or was it because a visit
abroad by. elther Herr Hitler or
Signor Mussolini was a thing so
rare? The two heads of, State,
have, however, met before but thelr
last meeting was not greeted with
the. same enthusiasm. 4
It is amazing, said Mr. Hodson,
how quickly some of us can forget
"the history of recent years. It
was not so long ago that the Nazi
‘putsch ‘in, Austria caused such 8
furore {n Italy and gave the Italian
» press in genera] an excellent op-
. portunity to burst forth in an antil-
German campaign. We do not
. -have_to.golback, further than 1934,
said’ the speaker, to.reallse. that
‘Herr Hitler and Signor Mussolin!,
despite thelr outside cordlality
_ were in fact quite hostile towards
‘each other, the main bone of con-
. tention being the future of Austria,
'THREE-CORNERED PACT.
It was in Marchi, 1934, one year

jatter the. German Chancellor had.’

‘come into power, that Italy
negotiated a three-cornered pact
with Austria and Hungary known
as fhe Rome protocols. These pro-
, tocols still hold good up to the
present though the background has
undergone a considerable ‘change.
The absorption of Austria, which

Hodson ;

was expected by those who follow-
ed events closely then, has" not
yet come about, due perhaps that
Nazi" propaganda in Vienna had
been kept in check.

Going on..to the invasion of
Abyssinia, Mr Hodson said that by
her actions in this particular case,
Italy had tacitly renounced the
Strassa principle: she joined Ger-
many in the revolt (eeainst the
order of things, “and this meant
that Italy hiad chosen the destiny
of being a”Mediterranean power
rather than a European one. ©

The eyes of Germany and Italy,
from a diplomatic point of view,
are now turned towards .different
- directions, The German policy ts
to keep Italy ag a Mediterranean
Power while Italy strives ‘to keep
German interest confined to Eas-
tern Europe and for this teason
the Rome-Berlin co-operation may.
continue indefinitely, for the thing
that binds these two countries to-
gether is not similarity of views,
but rather a distinct difference in
thelr political outlooks. :

Regardless of what critics might
say,.Germany does not wish to_be.
embroiled in any difficulties that
Italy might meet with in her Me-
diterranean activities and It {s
equally true that Italy desires to
be free. of any consequences that
might result’ from Germany’s eas-
tern European policy. As long as
there {s this buffer between the two
countries they will be able to con-
tinue the co-operation on the pre-
sent basis. Let it be remsmbered
that the fact alone that they are
both under dictatorships did not
prevent them from falling out in
1934, and it ts -not beyond the
bounds of possibility that they ‘will
| not fall out again.

am 4“

“ITALY | AND GERMANY

oe ‘Friendship And Co- “operation”

4

Berlin, Sept. - 28,

Herr Adolf Hitler, speaking’ here’
at the State Banquet in honour of
Signor Benito Mussolini, welcomed
Tl Duce saying: “At a time when

_ the world ts full of tension and
unrest,:in which there are very
dangerous elements attempting to
attack and destroy the old civilisa-
tion of Europe, Italy and Ger-
many find themselves in sincere
friendship and common political

’ go-operation, We belleve that our.

political .work, alming at the
securing of peace and the. high
florescence of European. civilisation

‘cannot be interpreted as the forma-
tion of a bloc directed against
other European States.

“In this spirit Italy and Ger-''

many will examine and deal with
the polltteal problems side by’ side
In order to oppose any attempts at
. Separating the two nations.” ;
Bignor Mussolini, replying, said
, since he entered Germany he had
felt around him everywhere the
spiritual atmagpnere of a great and
friendly’ people. <
te COMMON INTERESTS
“ "In: the “Fuehrer”
fighter who has given hack to the
German people a knowledge of its
‘greatness. J areet in him the

« ’

I greet a.

creator of a nation to which Fas-
cist Italy is tled in bonds of State
and Jabour.” ~'

“Italo-German solidarity is a
ilving, active solidarity. It will nob
result in political calculations, but
the expression and result of com-
mon interests. This solidarity, is
not, and won't be, a bloc, wrapped
up-in itself, which severs itself dis-
trustfully and ‘anxiously from the
rest of the world.

“Italy and Germany are prepared
to work together with all other
nations of good faith. What they
“demand is a respect ' ‘and under-

standing of thelr needs, necessities |.

and justified requirements.
.“The conversations 1 have had
with Your Excellency during the
last few days have strenghtened
‘our friendship against all attempts
to disturb and separate ug from
whichever side they may come.
“When I: return to Italy I shall
take with mea memory of your
powerful troops, your popular de-
monstrations and the. work of
physical and technical restoration
which the German nation has ful-

filled under {ts leader. Ahove all,

1 take back with me the definite
conyietion of our firm: friendship.”

—Reuter.

BISHOP'S ,OUTBURST..

' London, Sept. 28.
Dr. P. C.. Chang, of Nankai
University, arrived to- day from
Geneva on a mission to give the
Western peoples an eye-witness’s

; ‘account of the China war with

attention to the far reaching
issues involved. To-morrow’ he
addresses the Londdn Free Chur-
ches Federation the Central’ or-
ganisatlén of ‘the whole of the
British non-conformist body. On
Thursday he attends the inaugural.
meeting of the “Great China cam-
paign” at which Mr Norman
Angell, Mr. ‘Harold Laskl and Miss
Chu Chen-koo, Dr., Wellington
Koo's ‘daughter will speak,’ Dr.
Chang, will address the Labour
Party ‘conference at Bournemouth
on October 4. Interviews are also
being, arranged with "city and
political notabilities of all shades.
Chang - goes to
America on: October 6 and will re-
turn in time for the reopening of

-FRIGHTFULNESS

Strong Tendon
Comment

Hoos London, Sept. 28,

‘The London “Times’" in 9 leader
headed “Frightfulness” published
this morning, Bays It.is no easy
matter, for one nation to outrage
the feellngs. of the. whole world.
Japan, as yesterday's speeches at
Geneva made very plain, has for
past few days made herself the,|
exception to this rule. '

It has became terribly clear that
in prosecuting her undeclared war
with China she: ts resdived to
“abandon all. remorse.”
ror’s head. horrors accumulate,”
remarks the “Times,” and the
world watches her actions with
growing resentent and disgust.

_ LOFTY WORDS-—SHABBY
PENDANT.
Referring to the recent state-;
ment by Mr,, Yona! in the Japan-
ese Djet that it was unthinkable

4 the Japanese Imperial Navy would

commit any act contrary to hu-

‘| manity, the “Times” observes that

these lefty words make a shabby
navy's
latest exploit in systematically
destroying a whole fishing fleet of
junks. It is a crime revolting not
only -to-the -world-at-large, but to
those Japanese who still
honour the’ chivalrous traditions
‘of the Samural..

The tragedy of such wholesale
slaughter {s enhanced Incidentally
by the innumerable wounded, and
in this connection It Js sincerely to
be hoped that. sympathisers -{n

+ | Britain will be afforded the op-.

portunity ‘before’ many days. are
‘over of sending medical ass|stance
through ‘some recognised channel
to. these Innocent sufferers.

TACTICS OF BARBARISM

Japan, it seems, continues the
editorial, ig bent upon demoralls-
ing China hy methods which other
nations have not hesitated to con-
demn, ard which are,--indeed,
arousing . , public opinion, particu-
larly in this country and America
to a degree which Japan may well
have cause to regret. Her resort to
tactics of barbarism is a symptom
of her impatience, 1f not of her
alarm, and by making that her re-
sort she has conjured up a menace
‘ultimately greater than that, re-
presented by the elusive and in-
destructible Chinese soldier.
{a earning the unqualified con-
demnation of the world upon
whose good-will (whether she is at
peace, at war, or making good the
gains of "war) she vitally and In-
escapably depenis.

tish Government in concert with

DEATH — “DEAN
OF. WESTMINSTER)

“VERY REY. FOXLEY

NORRIS

London, Sept. 28.
The death has ‘occurred of
the Very. Rev. William Foxley
Norris, K.C.V.O., F.8.A., D.D., Hon.
ARLBA. Dean of Westminster
sihee 1925,,Dean of the Most Hon-
ourable Order of the Bath since
1926, and Hon. Chaplain, to. the :
Royal, Academy.—

Reuter’s Bulletin Service.

The Very Rev. Foxley Norris was
born on February 4, 1859, the. son
of the late Canon Foxley Norris.
He married. Mary Blanche Pott,
daughter of Archdeacon Pott
and they: have one son and two
daughters. .

The late Dean of Westminster
was cducated at Charterhouse,
Trinity College (Oxford) and Leeds
‘Clergy School, and was ordained
priest in. 1882. He subsequently
became Curate of Eton and of
Chatham, Vicar of Shirburn, Oxon,
Diocesan Inspector of Schools,
Vicar of Almondbury, Rector of
Barnsley, Canon. of Wakefield,
Archdeacon of Halifax, Chaplain to |
the High Sheriff-of Yorks, and was |
Denan'of York from 1917 to'1925. .

He was President of the Bradford
Arts Club, President of the Church
Reform League (1919-20), Director
of the Ecclesiastical Insurance
Company. “He.was Proctor in'Con-
vocation since 1894. He was, a
uater.. of palatine:

Japanese Government In Confusion

«

‘made by the Emperor, ‘In demo-
cratic countries, the right of mani-
pulating’ the constitution !s usually
vested, in the representative body
of the people. In Japan it becomes
the personal affair of the Emperor.
Moreover the Emperor has the ab-
solute power to dissolve, prorogue
the Diet thus the representative
body of the Japanese people only.
acts as a subservient organ, ="

UNLIMITED POWERS |

The powers of declaring war and
making treaties are the business at
the - Emperor, while the Diet may
be ‘Intormed as to the procedure
merely at the option of the Em-
peror. Added to the. above there
are many other Important powers
vested in the -Emperor, which
according to the practice of demo-
cratic government, should be In-'
herent “rights of the representative,
body. But after all, however omni-
potent the Mikado may appear to
be, it Is highly impossible for him
to, execute such a vast bundle of
rights without help from his sub-
1 ordinates. So the delegation of his
autocratic powers {8 indispensable.
’ Since the powers thus centralized
in the Emperor are so unquestion~
able and unimpeachable, the offi-
clals receiving : ‘the delegated powers

Emperor are undoubtedly and in-
Neldentally domineering, prejudicial
‘not only to the’ welfare: of the
¢ people but also against ‘the will of
| the Emperor. Granted that all the

wills and decisions of the Mikado.
could be so translated into actions
through the administration of his
subordinates, all the policies thus
arbitrarily manipulated by him are

all his decrees and ‘rescripts may.
not be all wise and judiclous. The
system | (of over- -riding the statutes |

to carry on.the government for.the ,

of the Diet by, the Imperial Ord- ' of the House of Peers. It is only

of Japanese constitutional govern-
ment. To say that there {s no rule
of law in Japan. ts not far {rom
tme. ;

a

THE HOUSE OF PEERS

Besides being ruled at the Mercy
ot their Majesty, the Japanese
‘people nave to put up with another
Leviathan,, namely the “House, of
Peers. Must of the second cham-
bers in democratic: governments
to-day have been reduced to such
4 cofidition that they are merely
acting in the capacity of an advi-
sory body !o the “Lowet , House.
They can bark, ~ but they cannot
bite, The House of Peers in Japan
Is the only exception. Whereas
the right of creating peers Is only
privileged.to the Emperor, and ft
cannot be dissolved at the will of
‘the Mikado, it has been given a
special status to check, embarass
and ‘harass the Lower House. This
principle was duly recognized. and
specified by Marquis Ito when he
framed the Constitution, Unlike’
the House of Lords in England
which 1s forced to give due respect
to the Budget Initiated by the
House of Commons, the House of
Peers possess stich a great power
as to throw out any budget pre-
sented by the Lower House, There
are many other ways in which the
House'of Peers can bring the work-

ing of the Lower House to a stale- |.

mate if it chooses to do 80. “with
this in view, ts {t not right to say
that the Japanese people are really
governed by. the Upper House?
Yet they ‘still claim they are a
democratic state,

When we say that Japan fs being

' ruled by the Upper House, we must

not go so far as to think that the |
Lower House !8 but the duplication

“FROM

“Anti-oplum work bristles with ]
diMculties, Firstly, the number of
opium addicts {s enormous; sec-
ondly, Governments in the Far
East sell oplum, and thirdly, tt is
thought “that opium revenue If
given up might atfect the whole
structure of the government’s

| nance” said Dr. ‘Chen Su Lan in

“Antt-
‘given

the course of a talk on
Opium. Work in Singapore”
at the Y.W.C.A. recently.

“There is a great deal of mis-
understanding about the work,” Dr.
Chen continued. "When I started
four or five years ago, I knew that
there were an’ enormous number
of oplum addicts in Britisn Maluya.
It frigntened me out of my wits
to think that there were then
about 400,000 addicts In this coun-
try out of a total population of
three and a half millions,

REGISTRATION INEFFECTIVE

“I had talks with the monopolies
people, and they disagreed with me
ag to that number, They sald that
it was not half as many as I cs-
timated, but you know, that the.
registration’ which is in force now
—tn fact, it has been tn force for
at least two or three years—in
most ineffective:

“Neither the police officers, nor
the monopolies peuplé, nor the
Gvernment can tell the number of

Special Period Of.

‘Training ©

Important changes In the method

ot promoting promising ‘young ,

seamen to commissioned rank In
the navy are announced by the
Admiralty. Encouragement to try
for a commission is to be glven to
all the boys under tralning for
the navy, both while they are ut
the naval schools ‘and’in their first
year or twp afloat. Those who are
marked out for possible selection
will be. specially .watehed by their
officers and as soon as they are
twenty years old can be ‘recom-
mended to & fleet selection board

| tor consideration. Those who are

accepted by the board will undergo
eight or nine ‘months’ special
training in a seagoing ship in the
same subjects 28 are taught’ to the
college-trained cadets.
ON LEVEL TERMS |.

The whole aim 1s .to put
the educational standard of the
“ranker’’ on a level with that of

the Dartmouth or = public-school,

cadet. The examination at the end
of the, training period, will be

similar to that. for the existing’

midshipmen; the candidates will
have to’ know the dutles of an

officer. of the Watch, the rule. of,

the. road, anchor work, general
organisation of a ship’s company,
and how to instruct tn boat work.
A boy getting 85 per cent of
marks will.be awarded a first~ctass
certificate. At least §0 per cent of
marks will be necessary to qualffy'at

| all. Those who pass will go before'|

a final selection board ‘and, it
accepted, will become acting sub-
Hevtenants. They will then under-

go a short course of instruction in
etiquette and social customs: and
will be advised .on questions of
uniform and dress, ’

WHAT IS: ‘PIRACY?

“what Is pray” take Professor
.|MeNatr, the well-known authority

on International Law, and he an-,
swers ‘the question himself by
saying that it is a crime under the
national law of every maritime
country, and in Britain it ts one .

of the four erlmes punishable ‘by

death, ”

According to International Law,
any country that catches Pirates |
in their waters may have the-
criminals’ tried by their own

courts, no matter to what nation- ”

ality the pirates belong. |

“But what ‘constitutes piracy?”
again asks the learned professor, ©
and again he answers it himself
thus: - “Tt isan act committed at
sea or at any rate close fo.the sea -
by persons not acting under le
ner authority.” »

Coming to the Mediterranean,
it is evident that the! pirate ships -
Operating there” “must belong to
one of the two partles_{ in confitct
in Spain, or else ‘they are. from.
some government. or governments
taking the part of either. one, of
the parttes. Tt, is intolerable that
neutral’ merchant shipping in the
Mediterranean " should be | Mnter-
fered with in.a manner which 1s
fur worse than ‘actual plracy.’:

WANTON
BOMBING
Of Non-Fc Fortified
_ Villages

200 CIVILIANS KILLED

Canton, eau, 28: Further’ de
talls of the wanton bombing nf
non-fortified vilinges in the in-
terior of the province by Japanese
planes yesterday ‘have Just, been
recelved here.

Three planes appearing trom the |

| south-east flew over Chingyuan, 60

ki'ometres north of Canton on the
North River, about 3.2) ‘a.m. and
attacked the town while - ihe
population was asleep. Five bombs
landed in the “most, densely
populated section of | the. town,
killing over;200 men, women and
‘children who had no opportunity
to escapt. Scores of houses have
been demolished. “

An investigation is . proceeding
and it ‘sg feared that the loss in

life and property will’ be much |

greater when completed.—

DEATH OF OLD.
CHINA -
COAST HAND

: CAPT, WB. B. HARDINGE

, One of the oldést and most
Interesting hands on the China
Coast passed away - yesterday
evening in the person of Captain
William Berkley Hardinge, who

‘died at his residence, No. 8 ‘Bay
View Mansion, at the age. of 89°

years

The late Captain Hardinge, who
was formerly -a pilot in Penang
lived and worked for many years
in the Far Hast, and up to ten
months ago was actively engaged
on river boats. He !s survived by
his wife and daughter, Mrs, Bralls-
ford, the wife of Mr.-A. Brallsford,
Wireless Sub-Engineer of the
Public ‘Works Department.

The ‘funeral takes place ‘this
afternoon passing” the Monument,
Happy Valley. at 5 p.m.

_ JAPANESE PLANES
OVER TUNGSHAN

‘Canton, Sept. 28,

- Chinese ‘officials informed Reuter
thut three Japanese planes flew
over Tungshan this morning, evl-
dently to observe the result of the
Japanese bombings of ‘the last few
days, Machine guns greeted the
visitors, causing no. damage. Mean-

| While forelgn observers state they

saw seaplanes dropping two bombs
on the milltary academy at Ying-
tong east of Canton where the
damage is alleged ‘to be small.—
Reuter. |

. WORLD MUST CALL
» A HALT., ©

Shanghai, Sept. 28.

“Whar we are seeing in China
to-aay we.wii see In ‘Europe to-
morrow unless the world will call
halt to Japan,” declared Mr, PF, L.
Kerran, the well-known British |
labour ledder and writer on arrival |

In, Shanghal from Nanking where |-

he witnessed the Japanese ralds’on
Nanking: last Saturday.

After the destruction and horror I
have seen, I sincerely hope at least
the labour movement will rally to
the cause of humanity. He added
I spent the most exciting ten days
of my life !n Nanking. Mr. Kerran
is investigating the condit‘ons in

‘the Far East.-~ Reuter,

CHOLERA-DEATHS IN
SHANGHAI te

Shanghai, Sept. 28,

Cholera deaths in the Chinese
refugee camps in Shanghai where
1853 cases have been recorded
have. passed the. 300 mark, There
\were 150 cases overnight which Js
the largest jump ‘since, the’ epide-
mic began. Testes :

"London, Sept. 28.

The Imperial Alrways flying boat
Cambria created a new retord
trans-Atlantic. fight when. she
landed at Foynes Harbour ten anda
half hours after leaving Botworth,
Newfoundland. This is the seventh!
filght of the flying boat which now
holds the record for both direc-
tions.—~
